</tr></table>
<p>where <em>A</em> is the feature vector extracted from one sensor comprising four elements, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq3"><math id="M18"><msub><mrow><mi>W</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></math></span> is the area surrounded by the response sensor and the windowing function curves, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq4"><math id="M20"><mrow><mi>R</mi><mo>(</mo><msub><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow></math></span> is the response value at the time <em>t</em><sub><em>k</em></sub>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq5"><math id="M22"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>K</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><msub><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow></math></span> is the value of the <em>i</em>-th windowing function at the time <em>t</em><sub><em>k</em></sub>, <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq6"><math id="M24"><mrow><mi>k</mi><mo>=</mo><mn>1</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>2</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>3</mn><mo>,</mo><mn>..</mn><mo>.</mo><mo>,</mo><mi>N</mi></mrow></math></span> denoting the sampling points of each curve, Δ<em>t</em> is the sampling interval between two sampling points, and the parameters (<span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq7"><math id="M26"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>a</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>b</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>,</mo><msub><mrow><mi>c</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub></mrow></math></span>) define the width, shape, and center, respectively, of the different windowing functions <span xmlns:mml="http://www.w3.org/1998/Math/MathML" id="IEq8"><math id="M28"><mrow><msub><mrow><mi>K</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>i</mi></mrow></msub><mo>(</mo><msub><mrow><mi>t</mi></mrow><mrow><mi>k</mi></mrow></msub><mo>)</mo></mrow></math></span><sup><a href="#CR63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR63">63</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="Par50">The data were cleaned by windowing phase with five windows, and various feature extraction methods (i.e., the maximum<sup><a href="#CR63" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR63">63</a></sup>, minimum<sup><a href="#CR60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR60">60</a></sup>, mean<sup><a href="#CR82" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR82">82</a></sup>, and median values<sup><a href="#CR60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR60">60</a></sup>) were performed in each window. All those four statistical parameters are the basic feature extraction methods obtained from sensor signals that have been extracted in the time domain<sup><a href="#CR60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR60">60</a></sup>. The maximum value indicates a change in the sensor maximum reaction rate in response to the scent of the sample. The minimum value is the lowest sensor response value of the entire data. The mean value is a comparison between the total number of sensor responses to the amount of data. The median value is the middle value of the overall sensor response. Maximum, minimum, mean, and median value equations are shown in Table <a href="#Tab5" class="usa-link">5</a><sup><a href="#CR60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR60">60</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="Par51">Time window slicing was implemented to create different window numbers (W0–W10) in the obtained signals. In case of no window (W0), the overall data were retrieved from 0 to 260 s. Meanwhile, for case of window number other than 0 (W1–W10), the data were retrieved from 20–200 s and subsequently divided into different parts with equal time span for each window.</p>
<p id="Par52">Feature extraction provides important information from a multidimensional sensor signal collection to obtain optimum results<sup><a href="#CR83" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR83">83</a></sup>. Basically, the purpose of feature extraction is to take the characteristics of the sample, so that the pattern recognition algorithm can easily recognize one sample from the others. After the features have been extracted from the sensor signals, chemometrics analysis was performed to classify the meat floss samples. Here, the learning investigation started with principal component analysis (PCA), which is an unsupervised pattern recognition method being able to reduce the dimensionality of the data by an orthogonal transformation into principal components (PCs), new variable sets (uncorrelated) that are linear with the original variable sets (possibly correlated variables)<sup><a href="#CR84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR84">84</a>–<a href="#CR86" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR86">86</a></sup>. Also, PCA is useful for observing the differences and similarities between various samples. In our case, only a few significant components are produced (e.g., PC1 and PC2) and subsequently plotted as a graph<sup><a href="#CR87" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR87">87</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="Par53">After performing unsupervised learning model of PCA, the analysis was continued with four supervised learning algorithms (i.e., linear discriminant analysis (LDA), quadratic discriminant analysis (QDA), k-nearest neighbors (k-NN), and random forest (RF)). LDA and QDA, which are boundary detection statistical methods, were used to verify the capability of the e-nose to correctly classify meat floss samples according to their types or origins (beef, chicken, and pork). LDA tries to find a linear combination of features and the best linear fit that can separate two or more sample groups<sup><a href="#CR84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR84">84</a></sup>. The LDA maximizes the distance of data between each group and minimize the distance of data in each group. LDA helps locate projection to maximize the separation between samples for class separation<sup><a href="#CR88" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR88">88</a></sup>. The projection is done by linearly transforming data from a high dimensional space to a low dimensional space, in which finally the decision is made in the low dimensional space<sup><a href="#CR89" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR89">89</a></sup>. The purpose of the LDA is to change the original dataset to a lower-dimensional space with good sample discrimination. Moreover, the possibility of overfitting is reduced<sup><a href="#CR87" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR87">87</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="Par54">The QDA is a simple algorithm and is different from LDA. QDA is used to find quadratic boundaries where a quadratic curve divides the variable space in regions. It computes the variance structures for each class separately, creating a more powerful discrimination rule for classes with different covariance matrices<sup><a href="#CR43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR43">43</a></sup>. QDA employs different variance-covariance matrices for each class, which differs it from LDA that only considers a single variance-covariance matrix for all classes<sup><a href="#CR90" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR90">90</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="Par55">The k-NN as a non-parametric method is used for classification, regression, and establishment of the classification model classes with different degrees of corruption<sup><a href="#CR73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR73">73</a>,<a href="#CR91" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR91">91</a></sup>. The main concept of k-NN is to provide a prediction target, calculate the distance or similarity between the predicted target and all samples, select the first <em>k</em> sample that is closest to each other, and finally use that sample to choose a decision according to the most votes of <em>k</em><sup><a href="#CR92" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR92">92</a></sup>. Here, <em>k</em> is a positive integer number of neighbors. To calculate the distance between the predicted targets, this model uses the Euclidean distance and selects the dominant class for it<sup><a href="#CR84" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR84">84</a>,<a href="#CR93" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR93">93</a></sup>. Euclidean distance is one of the statistical methods that can be used to calculate the interpoint distances between samples. The usage of the Euclidean distance is shown to be efficient for clustering data<sup><a href="#CR69" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR69">69</a>,<a href="#CR70" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR70">70</a></sup>. Using the closest suitable neighbor search algorithm, k-NN can become computationally traceable even for large datasets<sup><a href="#CR94" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR94">94</a></sup>.</p>
<p id="Par56">The RF model is a non-linear statistical ensemble method that constructs and subsequently averages many randomized, decorrelated decisions. RF utilizes the constructed model to estimate the content of samples by exploiting historical data<sup><a href="#CR64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR64">64</a></sup>. The method can be used not only for selecting variables (i.e., for understanding the important variables that are used for prediction), but also for solving classification and regression problems with strong abilities to eliminate the noise and to avoid overfitting<sup><a href="#CR64" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR64">64</a>,<a href="#CR73" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR73">73</a></sup>. In RF model, hyperparameter tuning is one of the methods utilized to configure the model parameters and increase the model performance. Nonetheless, a careful attention has to be given when applying this approach because hyperparameter tuning may lead to over-optimistic performance estimation if the used step-by-step methods are not precisely suitable for the model and data condition<sup><a href="#CR95" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR95">95</a></sup>. RF has several advantages in classification (i.e., the ability to be run on large and high dimensional datasets, good accuracy, stable prediction, and feature selection improvement)<sup><a href="#CR96" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR96">96</a></sup>. For regression tasks, the RF model has several advantages<sup><a href="#CR97" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR97">97</a></sup>. First, it offers an uncomplicated inclusion or exclusion of predictors based on data availability and user demands. Second, an inclusion of continuous and categorical predictors is possible. Third, it has a comparatively small number of model parameters that must be specified by the user. Fourth, the risk of overfitting can be minimized. Fifth, the automatic computation of a variable importance score is feasible to assess the contribution of individual predictors to the final model.</p>
<p id="Par57">For statistical analysis, the data matrix was normalized using scaling and centering techniques. After the normalization step, the initial database was divided into two groups (i.e., training and testing data). Training data were used to develop and create a classification model that allowed the best classification performance for the repeated k-fold cross validation (CV) procedure (10 folds x 10 repetitions, which ensured that at each validation run, 25% of the training data were used for validation accuracy). Meanwhile, the testing data were used for testing accuracy (full prediction) of the classification model that was previously created. The overall modeling development and analysis were carried out using open source R statistical software (version 3.5.1) and utilizing CARET, MASS, and the Kernlab library<sup><a href="#CR50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR50">50</a></sup>.</p></section><section id="Sec11"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Chemical compound characterization</h3>
<p id="Par58">FTIR spectroscopy can be used to qualitatively analyze the sample based on its functional groups<sup><a href="#CR98" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR98">98</a></sup>. In this work, it was used to rationalize the results of the e-nose analysis for distinguishing meat flosses based on their origins that are beef, chicken, and pork<sup><a href="#CR99" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR99">99</a></sup>. For FTIR analysis, the meat floss was dried separately in the oven for 7 min. The sample was ground into the powder and pelleted with KBr. The FTIR spectra were recorded on Shimadzu 21 Prestige FTIR spectrophotometer in the wavenumber range of 400–4000 cm<sup>−1</sup>.</p>
<p id="Par59">GC-MS analysis was performed to identify volatile compounds released by the samples<sup><a href="#CR100" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="CR100">100</a></sup>. It used a Chromeleon software, a carrier gas of ultra-high pure (UHP) helium, an injector temperature of 260 °C, a mobile phase flow rate of 50 mL/min, a split ratio of 50, a front inlet flow of 1 mL/min, a MS transfer line temperature of 250 °C, an ion source temperature of 200 °C, a purge flow of 3 mL/min, a gas saver flow of 5 mL/min, and a gas saver time of 5 min. For the GC-MS analysis, the meat floss had to be preprocessed first during sample preparation. The meat floss was dried in an oven and then ground into the powder. The sample was extracted using pure methanol (&gt;99%). The analysis was done at room temperature of 23 °C and relative humidity of 55%. The GC-MS data were recorded in an HP-5MS UI column with a length of 30 m and an inner diameter of 0.25 mm. Based on their ion molecular mass (M<sup>+</sup>) and fragmentation pattern, the molar mass of the compound was confirmed with the NIST 14 standard library.</p></section><section id="Sec12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Reporting summary</h3>
<p id="Par60">Further information on research design is available in the <a href="#MOESM2" class="usa-link">Nature Research Reporting Summary</a> linked to this article.</p></section></section><section id="sec13"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Supplementary information</h2>

</div></div></section></section></section><section id="ack1" class="ack"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Acknowledgements</h2>
<p>This work was partially funded by Hibah Penelitian Dosen Damas Alokasi Departemen Fisika FMIPA, Universitas Gadjah Mada (UGM) under grant agreement number of 133/J01.1.28/PL.06.02/2020. The authors thank all employees at PT Nanosense Instrument Indonesia for their administrative and technical support during the development and assessment of electronic nose.</p></section><section id="notes1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Author contributions</h2>
<p>H.S.W., K.T., and L.A.P. conceived the idea and concept, designed the study, formulated the materials, validated the methods, and interpreted the experimental results. H.S.W. and L.A.P. wrote the initial manuscript and revised the paper. L.A.P. performed the experiments and data collection using e-nose, implemented the machine learning models, and created the figures. I.R. and S.N.H. conducted learning computation and provided input for the data analysis. M.P. carried out the characterization and analysis of gas chromatography–mass spectrometry (GC-MS). A.R. analyzed the FTIR results and revised the paper. A.B.D. and S.W. validated the learning models and revised the paper. R.R. provided input on the chemical compounds of meat flosses. H.S.W. supervised the work, led the project, and acquired the funding at PT Nanosense Instrument Indonesia. K.T. supervised the work, led the project at Universitas Gadjah Mada (UGM), and acquired the funding. All authors have read and approved the final manuscript.</p></section><section id="notes2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Data availability</h2>
<p>The authors declare that all data supporting the findings of this study are available in the paper and supplementary information.</p></section><section id="FPar1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Competing interests</h2>
<p id="Par61">The authors declare no competing interests.</p></section><section id="fn-group1" class="fn-group"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Footnotes</h2>
